Theme Park Ride Design · Physics → Engineering → Story Interactive Field Guide

What Happens When…

A roller coaster is a gravity battery you ride: haul a train up a hill, and for the next ninety seconds it spends that stored energy as speed, airtime, and g-force, all choreographed by a steel curve surveyed to the millimetre. But a great ride is more than physics — it is a launch system and a block-zone safety brain, a fatigue-rated structure and a fleet of restraints, a story told in motion with a pre-show, a reveal, and a familiar face at the top of the drop. This guide builds ride engineering from first principles — energy and forces, track geometry, launches and magnetic brakes, structures and controls — then the whole lifecycle of design, testing, reliability, and maintenance, the craft of Imagineering and storytelling that makes a ride mean something, and the software that could help you design one. Grounded in how the world’s best parks actually do it, with live simulations of every mechanism.

01

Foundations — what a ride is, and the ideas everything rests on

Before the steel and the software, a ride is an idea: a choreographed sequence of sensations delivered to a human body, on demand, repeatably, and safely. This track builds that idea from first principles — what a ride actually is, the gravity-battery energy budget that powers nearly every coaster, the human tolerance envelope that bounds what you can deliver and the cravings you deliberately court, the thrill-safety-throughput-cost-story trade-off that every real ride negotiates, the vocabulary and subsystems that make a ride a system you can read, and the whole family of ride types you get to design from. Everything in the later tracks deepens one of these foundations.

01Foundations

What a ride actually is — a choreographed thrill inside a hard safety envelope

Analogy: A roller coaster is a piece of theatre performed by physics. A playwright scripts emotion beat by beat; a ride designer scripts sensation beat by beat — the lift that builds dread, the drop that empties your stomach, the snap of airtime — and then performs that exact script thousands of times a day, to the inch, without ever once hurting the audience.

Strip away the theming and a ride is a machine with one unusual product: a designed sequence of physical sensations, delivered to a human body, on demand, repeatably, safely. Everything in this volume serves that definition. The sensations are forces and motion — acceleration, airtime, the press of a turn — and the designer’s raw material is not steel or software but what the rider feels, and when. A ride is therefore closer to choreography or music than to a bridge: it has rhythm, pacing, a build and a release, a climax and a coda. The engineering exists to make that experience happen and keep happening.

Three hard constraints make this genuinely difficult, and they run through every track that follows. First, it must be safe — absolutely, every single cycle, for riders of wildly different sizes and states, with no exceptions tolerated; amusement rides are held to a safety standard closer to aviation than to consumer products. Second, it must be repeatable: the same forces, the same show, the same timing, cycle after cycle, for years, in heat and cold and rain. Third, it must move people — literally: a ride that thrills but serves 300 guests an hour is a commercial failure, so throughput is a design input, not an afterthought. Thrill, safety, and capacity pull against each other constantly (scenario 04).

What makes rides a uniquely rich engineering subject is that they sit at the intersection of almost every discipline at once. A single coaster is structural engineering (a fatigue-rated steel structure), dynamics (a vehicle on a 3-D path), mechatronics (launches, brakes, sensors, a safety-rated control system), human factors (biomechanics, comfort, anthropometry), show and media (theming, audio, lighting, effects), and operations (throughput, maintenance, reliability) — all wrapped around a story meant to make a stranger feel something. Few artefacts demand so many fields to cooperate so precisely.

So the mental model to carry through this guide: a ride is an experience, engineered. Every technical chapter — energy, forces, geometry, launches, structures, controls — is in service of a feeling delivered to a person, and every creative chapter — storytelling, Imagineering, guest experience — is constrained by what the physics and the safety case will allow. The great rides are the ones where those two halves are not in tension but in conversation: the engineering makes the story physical, and the story gives the forces meaning. Keep asking, of anything you design: what does the guest feel here, and is it safe, repeatable, and worth queuing for?

experience engineeredsafety enveloperepeatabilitythroughputmany disciplines at once
02Foundations

The gravity battery — why energy is the whole game

Analogy: A coaster is a savings account for energy. The lift hill is the one big deposit; every hill, loop, and turn after it is a withdrawal. You can spend the balance however you like — fast here, high there — but you can never spend more than you banked, and a little leaks out as ‘fees’ (friction) at every step. Understand the account and you understand the ride.

The single most important idea in coaster design is conservation of energy. At the top of the lift hill the train has a store of potential energy (PE) set purely by its height — PE = m·g·h. As it descends, that PE converts to kinetic energy (KE), the energy of motion — KE = ½·m·v² — and the train speeds up. Go back uphill and KE turns back into PE and it slows. The total (PE + KE) stays constant except for losses. This exchange is the engine of almost every coaster: the lift hill is the only place energy is added (on a traditional coaster), and from the crest onward the ride simply spends that fixed budget. The simulation shows it directly: raise the lift and the whole ‘energy ceiling’ rises, and with it the speed everywhere on the course.

Two consequences fall straight out of this and explain things you have seen without knowing why. First, the maximum speed is set by the drop height: at the bottom of the first drop, v ≈ √(2·g·h) — a 50 m drop gives roughly 110 km/h, no matter the train, because the mass cancels out. Second, every subsequent hill must be lower than the last: the train can only rise as high as the energy it has left, and since friction has skimmed some off, each crest sits a little below the one before. A layout is, quite literally, a staircase of descending energy — which is why coaster profiles always trend downward from the first drop.

The ‘fees’ are friction and air resistance, and they matter enormously (the next track is devoted to them). Every wheel, every bend, every metre through the air skims a little energy as heat and sound, so the real energy ceiling decays across the course — the dashed line in the simulation. Design too close to that ceiling and the train ‘valleys’: it runs out of energy and stalls in a dip (a rollback or valley), the ride-stopping embarrassment every designer fears. So every element is sized with a margin below the available energy, and on a cold morning (stiffer grease, denser air) that margin shrinks — which is why rides behave differently at open versus mid-afternoon.

The beautiful part is that mass mostly cancels out of the speeds and forces, so the feel of a coaster is governed by its geometry and heights, not by how heavy the train is — which is exactly what lets a designer sketch a ride’s whole character as an energy diagram before a single piece of steel exists. The reflex to build: think of a ride as a fixed energy budget, deposited once and spent down a decaying ceiling. A good self-check — given a 60 m first drop, roughly how fast at the bottom, and could a later hill be 55 m tall? (About 123 km/h; and no — friction means the second hill must sit a few metres under 60, let alone 55.)

The lift hill is one deposit; every later hill spends it down an energy ceiling that friction keeps lowering

Raise the lift and the whole course speeds up; every crest must sit under the red energy ceiling. Add friction and the ceiling sinks faster — push a later hill too close to it and the train valleys (rolls back). Mass cancels, so geometry and height set the feel.

03Foundations

What the body can take — and what it craves

Analogy: A great chef works between two lines: too bland to enjoy, and too intense to stomach. A ride designer works the same narrow band with forces — below it the ride is dull, above it it is dangerous or sickening — and the whole art is dancing along the top edge of pleasure without crossing into harm.

Every sensation a ride delivers is, physically, an acceleration — a change in speed or direction — felt by the body as a g-force (multiples of normal gravity). Push up through a valley and you feel heavy (positive g, blood drains toward your feet); crest a hill and you feel light or lifted (negative g, ‘airtime’); swing through a turn and you feel shoved sideways (lateral g). The body has a well-mapped tolerance envelope for these: sustained positive g beyond roughly 4–6 can grey-out or red-out vision as blood shifts; strong negative g is disorienting and hard on the neck; lateral g is uncomfortable and tiring. Rides live comfortably inside this envelope, using brief peaks (a fraction of a second) where sustained loads would be unsafe — the forces track makes this precise.

But there is a second, subtler quantity that separates a thrilling ride from a punishing one: jerk, the rate of change of acceleration. It is not how much g you pull but how suddenly it arrives. A force that ramps in smoothly reads as exhilarating; the same force slammed on reads as a jolt, a wrench, a whiplash. This is why a well-designed element eases into and out of its loads (the clothoid and transition geometry of the track track), and why a badly-designed one — or a worn one — feels ‘rough.’ Managing jerk is much of what makes engineering feel like craft: two rides can pull the same peak g and feel worlds apart in comfort.

The craving side is just as real and just as designed. The sensations people seek — the floating lift of airtime, the controlled fear of a big drop, the rush of speed, the disorientation of an inversion — are pleasurable precisely because they are safe simulations of danger: the body’s alarm systems fire (adrenaline, a stomach lurch) while the thinking brain knows it is fine, and that gap is the thrill. A designer is, in effect, playing the rider’s nervous system like an instrument — building anticipation on the lift, delivering release on the drop, surprising with a hidden element, resolving with a smooth finish. The forces are the notes; the sequencing is the music.

Crucially, the envelope is not one-size-fits-all, which shapes everything downstream. Riders span children to adults, slight to large, and the same forces act differently on different bodies — which is why restraints, seat geometry, and height/health requirements exist, and why ‘comfort’ is engineered for the range of the audience, not an average. The reflex: design forces by their peak, their duration, and their rate of onset, kept inside the human envelope for every body in the queue, while deliberately courting the sensations people came for. A self-check: why can a ride briefly hit 5 g safely but not hold 5 g for ten seconds? (Duration — the body tolerates brief spikes that sustained loads would not, because blood takes time to pool.)

g-force envelopepositive / negative / lateral gjerk (rate of onset)brief peaks vs sustainedsafe simulation of danger
04Foundations

The impossible triangle — thrill, safety, throughput (and cost, and story)

Analogy: Running a kitchen, you can have fast, cheap, or excellent — pick two. Designing a ride is that trade-off with higher stakes and more corners: crank the thrill and you may bruise capacity or budget; maximise throughput and you may blunt the story; and safety is the one corner that is never, ever traded. Every real ride is a negotiated settlement between these pulls.

A ride is a system of competing objectives, and understanding the tensions is what turns ‘cool idea’ into ‘buildable ride.’ The headline trio: thrill (the intensity and quality of the experience), safety (non-negotiable, and expensive to guarantee), and throughput (guests per hour, which pays for everything). They genuinely fight. A more intense ride often means longer, more complex, with heavier restraints that take longer to check — cutting capacity. A higher-capacity ride wants fast loading, simple restraints, short cycles — which can cap how wild it gets. And every safety margin, redundancy, and inspection adds cost and sometimes cycle time. There is no free lunch; there is only a chosen balance.

Two more corners pull on the triangle and are easy to underweight. Cost — capital (steel, systems, theming can dwarf the ride itself) and operating (power, staff, maintenance, downtime) — bounds everything; the most thrilling concept that cannot be built or run within budget is not a design, it is a daydream. And story / experience — especially at the top destination parks — is often the point, and it competes for the same time and money: a slow, immersive dark ride and a short, punchy coaster are different answers to ‘what is this ride for?’ A family boomerang coaster, a record-breaking hypercoaster, and a trackless story ride are not better or worse — they are different settlements of the same constraints.

The discipline that makes this tractable is deciding the ride’s intent first and letting it rank the trade-offs. Is this a capacity ride (a park’s workhorse that must eat queues all day), a signature thrill (a record or a headline that defines the park), a family ride (gentle, broad audience, high re-rideability), or a story ride (immersion over intensity)? That intent tells you where to spend and where to compromise — a workhorse optimises dispatch and reliability over peak thrill; a signature coaster accepts lower capacity for a world-first sensation. Designers who skip this step build rides that are mediocre at everything because they tried to be best at all of it.

Safety sits outside the trade space, and that distinction is the professional’s bright line: thrill, throughput, cost, and story are negotiated; safety is satisfied, always, first, as a precondition, never balanced against the others. Everything else is optimised within the space that safety allows. The reflex: name the ride’s primary intent, then consciously trade thrill, throughput, cost, and story against each other — and never against safety. A self-check: a park needs its new coaster to clear a 2,000-person queue on peak days and be its most intense ever — where will that tension show up, and what might give? (In cycle time and restraint checking — the intensity pushes toward complex restraints and long cycles, which fight the capacity target; something — train count, element count, or the capacity goal — must flex.)

Watch: the thrill-safety-throughput triangle
competing objectivessafety is non-negotiablethroughput pays for itcost bounds everythingdecide the ride’s intent first
05Foundations

Anatomy of a ride — the vocabulary and the systems that make one

Analogy: Learning a ride is like learning a body: once you know that there’s a skeleton, muscles, a nervous system, and a circulatory system — and what each does — the whole thing stops being a blur and becomes a set of named parts working together. Here is the anatomy, so the rest of this guide reads as recognition, not vocabulary soup.

A coaster’s experience is built from elements — the named moves that make up the choreography: the lift (or launch) that supplies energy; drops (the stomach-droppers); hills or camelbacks (airtime); inversions (loops, corkscrews, zero-g rolls — going upside down); turns and helices (sustained lateral force); s-bends and transitions; and brake runs and the station. The ordered sequence of these elements is the layout — the ride’s script. Designers speak fluently in these terms because each element has a characteristic force signature, and composing a ride is largely choosing and sequencing elements so the forces build, vary, surprise, and resolve without ever becoming monotonous or punishing.

Underneath the elements sit the physical subsystems, which map onto the engineering tracks ahead. The track (the precise 3-D path, the geometry that is the ride) and its supports and structure (holding it up against big dynamic loads). The ride vehicles — trains or cars, their chassis, their wheels (running, up-stop, and side/guide wheels that lock them to the track), and their restraints. The drive systems — lifts, launches, and brakes — that add and remove energy. And, pervasively, the control and safety system: the sensors, logic, and interlocks that run the ride, enforce block zones, and bring everything to a safe stop when anything is wrong. These are the organs; the elements are the behaviour they produce.

Around the mechanical ride wraps the experience layer, which at the great parks is half the project: theming and environment (the built world the ride lives in), show systems (animatronics, audio, lighting, projection, special effects, all synchronised), the queue and pre-show (where the story and the wait are designed), and the station and loading experience. And binding it all is the operational layer: how the ride is dispatched, staffed, inspected, and maintained — because a ride is not a product you ship once but a machine you run, safely and profitably, for decades.

Holding these layers in mind — experience (elements) → mechanism (track, vehicle, drives, controls) → show → operations — is what lets you read any ride, existing or imagined, as a coherent system rather than a jumble. The reflex: when you look at a ride, name its elements, then trace the subsystems that produce them, then the show that dresses them, then the operation that runs them. A self-check: a train crests a hill and you float out of your seat — name the element, the subsystems that make it happen and keep you safe, and the operational concern it raises. (Element: a camelback / airtime hill. Subsystems: track geometry sets the negative-g crest, the vehicle’s up-stop wheels and your restraint keep you attached, the structure carries the load. Operation: that restraint must be checked every dispatch — a throughput cost.)

Watch: the anatomy and vocabulary of a ride
elementslayouttrack / structurevehicle / restraints / drivescontrol & safety system
06Foundations

The family of rides — coasters, dark rides, flat rides, and everything between

Analogy: Rides are a family tree, not a single species. Coasters are the loud, athletic cousins; dark rides are the storytellers; flat rides are the compact acrobats; and the modern hybrids borrow from all of them. Knowing the family — what each branch is for and how it works — is what lets you reach for the right kind of ride for a given job, and recognise when a ‘new’ ride is really a clever cross.

The broadest split is by how the ride moves you and what it is for. Roller coasters are gravity-and-momentum machines on a fixed track — the physics playground of this volume — ranging from gentle family coasters to launched hypercoasters and multi-inversion thrill machines. Dark rides carry guests slowly through themed, show-filled environments on a vehicle (tracked, or increasingly trackless), prioritising story, immersion, and animatronics over force — the ride is the scenes it passes through. Flat rides are the compact, often spinning or swinging machines (carousels, wave swingers, pendulums, spin-and-pukes, drop towers) that deliver concentrated sensation in a small footprint with high throughput. And water rides (log flumes, rapids, splash coasters) use water for both the thrill and the cooling.

Cutting across those categories are motion-based and media rides, the fastest-growing branch and a major gap-filler versus a pure coaster: a vehicle on a motion base (often a 6-DOF platform — the motion-base simulation) synchronised to a screen or physical scenes, so a modest physical movement, perfectly timed to what the eyes see, ‘feels’ like soaring, falling, or racing. These trade raw g-force for illusion and story, can re-theme by swapping media, and are central to modern IP-driven attractions. Alongside them sit drop towers, observation rides, powered coasters (motor-driven rather than gravity), and a growing menagerie of hybrids.

The most important modern trend is convergence: the branches are blending. A ride can be a coaster and a dark ride (a story-driven ‘dark coaster’ with launches, scenes, and show in the dark); trackless vehicles bring dark-ride freedom to new choreography; robotic arm ride systems put riders on the end of an industrial robot for motions no track could make; and media, motion bases, and physical sets combine so thoroughly that ‘what kind of ride is this?’ stops having a single answer. The leading attractions at top parks are usually hybrids precisely because combining branches lets designers get thrill and story and capacity in ways a pure form cannot.

For a designer, the family tree is a palette: each branch has a native strength (coasters for force, dark rides for story, flat rides for footprint and capacity, motion/media for illusion and re-theming) and native costs, and the craft is choosing — or combining — the right branch for the ride’s intent (scenario 04). The reflex: match the ride type to the job — and consider a hybrid when no single branch gives thrill, story, and capacity together. A self-check: a park wants a high-capacity, deeply story-driven, re-themeable attraction tied to a film franchise, with moderate (not extreme) physical intensity — which branch(es) fit? (A trackless and/or motion-base dark ride, very likely media-rich — story and re-theming and capacity over raw g; a pure coaster would fight all three goals.)

The foundations, in one breath —A ride is an experience, engineered: a scripted sequence of forces delivered to a human body — powered by a fixed energy budget (gravity battery), kept inside the human tolerance envelope (peak, duration, and jerk), balanced across thrill / safety / throughput / cost / story (safety never traded), built from named elements on a stack of subsystems, and drawn from a whole family of ride types you can mix. Every later track deepens one of these.

02

Coaster physics — energy, friction, speed, and force from first principles

A gravity coaster is pure mechanics made thrilling, and this track builds that mechanics from the ground up so the rest of the engineering has firm footing. It covers the two energies and their endless exchange, the friction and drag that tax every metre and shape the always-descending layout, the speed-from-height rule v = √(2gh) and why you cannot cheat it, the momentum and inertia that make the train resist every change and turn those changes into the forces riders feel, the centripetal force a = v²/r behind every curve and loop, and finally the analytic habit of reading (and sketching) any ride as two overlaid diagrams — a decaying energy budget and the force profile the geometry extracts from it.

07Coaster physics

The two energies and their exchange — potential, kinetic, and the dance between them

Analogy: Think of a swing. At the top of its arc it is still for an instant, all ‘stored-up height’; at the bottom it is a blur, all motion; and it trades one for the other, back and forth, forever (minus a little loss). A coaster is one long, elaborate swing — the same two energies, the same trade, just choreographed into drops and hills instead of a simple arc.

Potential energy (PE) is energy a body has because of its position — for a coaster, its height: PE = m·g·h, where m is mass, g is gravity (9.81 m/s²), h is height. Lift a train up and you are literally pumping energy into it and storing it as height; it is ‘loaded’ like a drawn bow. Kinetic energy (KE) is energy a body has because of its motion: KE = ½·m·v², where v is speed. Note the square — doubling the speed quadruples the kinetic energy — which is why high speeds are so ‘expensive’ in energy terms and why the fastest coasters need either enormous drops or powered launches to reach them. These two quantities, and the trade between them, are the entire mechanical story of a gravity coaster.

The exchange is continuous and lossless in the ideal: at every instant, whatever height the train gives up becomes speed, and whatever speed it gives up becomes height, so PE + KE = constant. Concretely: on the lift, you add PE (height up, speed low). Crest the first drop and PE pours into KE (height falls, speed rockets — maximum speed at the lowest point). Climb the next hill and KE pours back into PE (speed bleeds off, height climbs). Over a loop, the train is slowest at the top (high PE) and fastest at the bottom (high KE). If you could watch a running total of PE + KE, it would be a flat line — the budget from scenario 02, now seen instant by instant.

This immediately explains features that otherwise seem arbitrary. Why the first drop is the biggest: it is where the most PE is available to convert, so it produces the ride’s top speed — everything after works with what is left. Why loops get smaller toward the top and the whole layout descends: the train must always have enough KE to keep moving (and, in a loop, enough to maintain contact and positive force at the top), so elements are sized against the energy still in the account. Why a heavier train is not a slower train: mass appears in both PE and KE and cancels in the speed equation — a full train and an empty one reach nearly the same speed (friction and air resistance, which do not scale cleanly with mass, cause the small real differences).

The deep intuition to hold: a coaster is a machine for trading height and speed, and a designer composes a ride by deciding where to spend the budget as height (for a slow, high, floaty moment) and where to spend it as speed (for a fast, intense one) — always within the fixed total. The reflex: read every point on a coaster as a split of one constant total between height and speed. A self-check: at the top of a vertical loop the train is moving but slow; at the bottom it is fast — where is the energy mostly PE, and where mostly KE, and why must the train still be moving at the top? (Top: mostly PE, little KE — but it must keep enough KE to maintain speed and, with the track, the force that holds it on the rails; bottom: mostly KE, PE near zero.)

Watch: potential and kinetic energy
PE = mghKE = ½mv²continuous exchangev² makes speed expensivemass cancels
08Coaster physics

Friction and drag — the tax on every metre, and the energy budget

Analogy: A real swing does not swing forever — each arc is a little lower than the last, until it hangs still. That slow decay is friction and air resistance quietly skimming energy on every pass. A coaster pays the same tax, and the whole shape of a layout — always trending downhill — is the visible shadow of that steady leak.

The ideal ‘PE + KE = constant’ is a useful fiction; the real world charges losses, and they set the character and the limits of a ride. Two main taxes apply. Friction: wheels deforming and rolling on the track, bearings turning, flanges rubbing — mechanical contact that turns motion into heat. Air resistance (drag): pushing the train and its riders through air, which rises steeply with speed (roughly with v²), so it dominates at high speed — a hypercoaster spends a large share of its budget just parting the air. Together these mean the real total energy decays along the course — the descending ‘energy ceiling’ of the simulation — and every element must sit below the ceiling as it is at that point, not as it started.

The headline consequence is the one everyone notices without naming: a gravity coaster always, on balance, runs downhill. It must, because each element returns slightly less energy than it borrowed, so it can never climb back to its previous height. The layout is a staircase descending from the first drop, and the designer’s job is to spend the decaying budget so cleverly that the ride stays exciting all the way to the brakes — big moments early while energy is plentiful, cannily-sized moments late. Push a late element too high or too fast and you risk the valley: the train arrives with too little energy to clear a crest, stalls in a dip, and rolls back — the simulation shows exactly where that failure appears as friction rises.

Friction is not purely an enemy; it is also how you stop and control the ride on purpose, which is the bridge to the brakes track. Brake runs deliberately dump the remaining KE (friction brakes press pads on a fin; magnetic brakes induce drag without contact) to slow the train safely. And friction is variable in ways that matter operationally: it rises when grease is cold and stiff (morning), when bearings are worn, when the air is dense, when the train is light (fewer riders, less momentum to overcome fixed drag) — which is why the first trains of the day, and lightly-loaded trains, run slower and are the ones most at risk of valleying. Designers build an energy margin precisely to absorb this variation.

The design reflex: treat friction and drag as a tax you must budget for, size every element under the decaying ceiling with margin, and remember the margin is smallest when it is cold and the train is light. It is also why reducing losses — better wheels, bearings, and aerodynamics — is valuable: it raises the ceiling, letting a given lift height do more. A self-check: a new coaster runs perfectly on a warm, busy afternoon but a lightly-loaded train stalls in a mid-course hill on a cold morning — what happened, and what are two fixes? (Cold stiff grease and a light train raised friction and lowered momentum, so the decaying energy ceiling dipped below that hill’s crest — a valley. Fixes: lower the problem hill, add energy margin elsewhere, warm/condition the track before opening, or restrict very light dispatches.)

Each hill lower than the last; raise the friction and the train runs out of budget and valleys

Every hill returns slightly less than it borrowed, so the layout descends. Turn friction up and the energy ceiling drops faster — eventually a crest sits above what is left and the train stalls (valley / rollback). The green/red dots show exactly which crests it can still clear.

09Coaster physics

Speed from height — √(2gh), and why you cannot cheat it

Analogy: Drop a stone from a tower and its speed at the bottom depends only on how high the tower was — not on the stone’s weight, not on how you let go. A coaster train on its first drop obeys the same iron rule: the drop height alone sets the speed at the bottom, which is why a designer can read a ride’s top speed straight off its tallest drop.

Set the ideal PE and KE equal — all the height converts to speed — and the mass cancels, leaving one of the most useful equations in the field: v = √(2·g·h), the speed gained from a drop of height h (ignoring losses). It is worth building a feel for the numbers: a 10 m drop gives about 14 m/s (50 km/h); 20 m gives ~20 m/s (71 km/h); 50 m gives ~31 m/s (113 km/h); 100 m gives ~44 m/s (159 km/h). Notice the diminishing returns — because speed grows with the square root of height, doubling the drop does not double the speed (it multiplies it by √2 ≈ 1.41). To go twice as fast from gravity alone you need four times the drop — which is exactly why the race for ever-higher top speeds eventually abandoned taller hills for launches (the drive track).

This equation is the designer’s back-of-envelope superpower: given a target top speed, it tells you the drop height you need (invert it: h = v²/(2g)), and given a drop, it tells you the speed to expect — before any detailed simulation. It also sets up the forces downstream: the speed at the bottom of the drop feeds straight into the g-force of the pull-out that follows (forces and geometry tracks), so the drop height is quietly deciding not just how fast but how hard the ride hits. Real speeds come out a little lower than √(2gh) because of the friction and drag tax, but the equation gives the ceiling and the right mental model.

The ‘cannot cheat it’ part is important and liberating. Because the result depends only on height (and g, which you cannot change), there is no clever trick of track shape or train design that gets more speed out of a given drop — a straight plunge and a curvy one of the same vertical drop reach the same bottom speed (the curvy one just takes a different path and feels different on the way). What track shape does control is the forces and the feel (how suddenly the speed arrives, what g you pull), not the final speed. Separating ‘how fast’ (set by height) from ‘how it feels’ (set by geometry) is one of the clarifying insights of coaster physics.

The reflex: read top speed straight off the biggest drop with v = √(2gh), respect the square-root diminishing returns, and reach for a launch when height stops paying. A self-check: a park wants a 150 km/h coaster. Roughly how tall a drop would pure gravity need, and why might they launch instead? (150 km/h ≈ 41.7 m/s; h = v²/(2g) ≈ 1740/19.6 ≈ 89 m — an ~90 m drop, a very tall and costly structure; a launch reaches that speed on flat ground, saving the tower — which is why most modern speed records are launched, not dropped.)

Watch: speed from height — √(2gh)
v = √(2gh)mass-independentsquare-root diminishing returnsheight sets speed, geometry sets feellaunches beat tall drops
10Coaster physics

Momentum and inertia — the train wants to keep going (and going straight)

Analogy: A loaded shopping trolley at a jog is hard to stop and hard to swerve — it ‘wants’ to keep rolling, and keep rolling straight. A coaster train is that trolley with tonnes of mass and a lot of speed: its reluctance to change speed or direction (inertia) is exactly what the track, the brakes, and the restraints spend their lives fighting — and what the rider feels as force.

Momentum (mass × velocity, p = m·v) and inertia (a body’s resistance to any change in its motion — Newton’s first law) are the other half of coaster mechanics, complementary to energy. Energy tells you how fast the train can go; momentum and inertia tell you how hard it is to change what it is doing — to speed it up, slow it down, or bend its path. A multi-tonne train at 100 km/h carries enormous momentum, and every component that alters its motion must supply a correspondingly large force: the launch to get it moving, the brakes to stop it, and — crucially — the track itself to keep redirecting it around curves it would otherwise fly straight past.

The ‘keeps going straight’ part is the key to understanding forces. By inertia, the train wants to travel in a straight line at constant speed; any deviation — a curve, a hill, a dip — requires a force to compel it, and by Newton’s third law the train (and your body) push back just as hard. That reaction is what you feel: the track pushes you up through a valley (you feel heavy — positive g), the restraint and seat pull you down over a crest (or you’d keep going straight up — airtime), the rail shoves you sideways through a turn (lateral g). Every g-force a rider feels is inertia resisting a change of motion the track is imposing. This is the physical root of the entire forces track.

Inertia also governs the time and distance scales that shape a layout. You cannot change a train’s speed or direction instantly — it takes distance to stop (why brake runs are long), distance to turn without brutal force (why tight turns at speed hurt — large momentum change in a short path = huge force), and smooth transitions to ease between elements without a jolt (the jerk problem from scenario 03). The faster and heavier the train, the more room every change needs. This is why high-speed sections are drawn with large, sweeping radii and why designers think constantly about the rate at which they ask the train to change what it is doing.

The reflex: treat the train as a massive object that resists every change of motion — and read each rider sensation as that resistance pushing back. It unifies the ride: energy sets the speeds, inertia turns those speeds into the forces and the space every element needs. A self-check: two turns have the same radius, but one is taken at 40 km/h and the other at 80 km/h — how do the sideways force and the room needed compare, and why? (The force scales with v², so doubling the speed quadruples the sideways force for the same radius — the fast turn either needs a far larger radius or heavy banking to stay comfortable; momentum makes speed expensive in force, just as it is in energy.)

Watch: momentum and inertia
momentum p = mvinertia (Newton 1)force = imposed change of motionevery g is inertia pushing backchanges need time & distance
11Coaster physics

Centripetal force — what it takes to bend a path, and where it comes from

Analogy: Whirl a ball on a string in a circle and your hand feels the constant inward pull needed to keep it curving; let go and the ball flies off straight. Every curve, loop, and turn on a coaster is that string — the track supplies a constant inward pull to keep bending the train’s path, and the strength of that pull is the force the rider feels.

To move in a circle (or any curve) at speed, a body needs a continuous force directed toward the centre of the curve — the centripetal force — because otherwise inertia carries it straight off on a tangent. Its size is F = m·v²/r, or as an acceleration (what sets the g-force), a = v²/r: proportional to the square of speed and inversely to the radius r of the curve. This single relation is the engine of the forces and geometry tracks. Read it as intuition: fast + tight = brutal (high v, small r → huge force); fast + sweeping = gentle (high v, large r → modest force). It is why high-speed elements are drawn large and why a small radius at speed is the signature of a painful, or unsafe, moment.

The v² is the part that catches people out and deserves a feel. Because the force grows with the square of speed, doubling the speed quadruples the force for the same curve. A turn that is pleasant at 40 km/h delivers four times the sideways push at 80 km/h — which is exactly why a designer cannot simply reuse a turn shape at higher speed, and why the energy-rich, high-speed parts of a layout (near the bottom of the first drop) demand the largest radii. The r in the denominator is the designer’s main lever: to keep a given element’s force comfortable as speed rises, open up the radius; to make a slow element feel punchy, tighten it.

Where the centripetal force comes from depends on the element, and it maps directly onto what the rider feels (the forces track makes this precise). At the bottom of a valley, the track pushes up harder than gravity to bend the path upward — the rider feels heavy (positive g > 1). At the top of a crest, gravity alone may supply the needed inward (downward) force, or more than enough — the rider feels light or lifted (airtime, g < 1 or negative). In a flat turn, the rail pushes sideways — lateral g. In a loop, the needed force and gravity combine differently at every point, which is why loop shape is so carefully engineered (the clothoid, geometry track). The force is always real; only its source and direction change.

The reflex: for every curve, think F = m v²/r — square the speed, divide by the radius, and that is the force the track must supply and the rider will feel. It is the one equation that connects energy (which set the speed) to sensation (which the geometry shapes). A self-check: a designer wants the same gentle 2 g at the bottom of two different drops, one reaching 60 km/h and one reaching 120 km/h — how must the pull-out radii compare? (Force ∝ v²/r, so to hold the same g at double the speed you need four times the radius — the faster drop’s pull-out must be far larger and sweeping to feel the same, a core reason big coasters look the way they do.)

Watch: centripetal force and curves
centripetal forcea = v²/rfast + tight = brutalradius is the leversource changes, force is real
12Coaster physics

The coaster as a physics diagram — reading and sketching a ride in energy and force

Analogy: A musician can look at a score and hear it; a coaster engineer can look at a layout and feel it — where the speed peaks, where the airtime floats, where the g piles on — because the whole ride is legible as two overlaid diagrams: the energy budget and the force profile. Learn to read (and sketch) those two, and you can design a ride on paper before touching steel.

Everything in this track combines into a single analytic habit: see a ride as two curves plotted along its length. The first is the energy / speed profile — from conservation and friction, the train is fastest at low points and slowest at high ones, with the whole thing descending as losses accrue (the first two simulations). The second is the force profile — from centripetal force and gravity, a trace of the g felt at each moment: positive-g spikes at valley bottoms and loop bottoms, negative-g dips at crests (airtime), lateral pushes in turns. These two diagrams are the ride: the first tells you what is physically possible at each point (you only have the energy you have), and the second tells you what it will feel like (the sensation the geometry extracts from that energy).

Reading an existing ride this way is the fastest route to understanding it. Spot the tall first drop → expect the top speed just after it (√(2gh)) and a strong positive-g pull-out (tight radius) or a gentle one (sweeping). Spot a sharp crest at speed → expect ejector airtime (small r, v²/r > g upward). Spot a high, slow hill late in the layout → expect floaty airtime and know the designer was spending dwindling energy as height. Spot a tight turn → check the speed there and estimate the lateral g; if it is fast and tight, expect either heavy banking or an uncomfortable moment. The physics lets you predict the feel from the shape, which is the heart of design review.

Sketching a new ride runs the same logic forward. Start with the ride’s intent and target sensations (foundations), set the first-drop height to hit the top speed you want (h = v²/2g), then lay out elements left to right, at each step checking: do I still have the energy for this (is it under the decaying ceiling)? and does its radius-and-speed give the force I want (a = v²/r), smoothly reached (jerk)? You iterate — raise a hill here, open a radius there, add a trim brake to tame a too-fast section — shaping the two curves until the ride reads the way you intend. This is precisely the loop the ride-design software track automates (and the ridesim simulation previews).

The reflex, and the summation of coaster physics: design and critique rides as two overlaid diagrams — a descending energy/speed budget, and a force profile the geometry extracts from it — and iterate the shape until both read right. Master this and the later engineering tracks become detail on a structure you already understand. A self-check: sketch, in words, the energy and force story of a classic out-and-back airtime coaster — lift, big first drop, then a series of camelback hills to the brakes. (Energy: deposit at the lift, top speed at the first drop’s base, then a descending series of hills each lower than the last as friction bleeds the budget. Force: strong positive g in the first pull-out, then a rhythm of negative-g airtime pops over each camelback crest, fading in strength as speed drops, until the brakes dump what is left.)

Coaster physics, in one breath —A gravity coaster is energy (a fixed budget, deposited at the lift, traded between height and speed, decaying under friction & drag, giving v = √(2gh)) made to produce forces (inertia resisting every change the track imposes, centripetal a = v²/r at every curve). Read any ride as two diagrams — a descending energy/speed budget and the force profile the geometry extracts from it.
Watch: reading a ride as a physics diagram
energy / speed profileforce / g profilepredict feel from shapeiterate the two curvesdesign on paper first
03

Forces & the body — the sensations, their limits, and their arrangement

Forces are the raw material of thrill, and this track is about what the track does to the human body and how a designer shapes it into pleasure. It covers positive g (the heavy moments, rationed by duration because blood obeys physics), airtime (float to ejector, where the restraint becomes load-bearing), lateral g (the disliked sideways force, banked away or kept as a measured ‘wild’ kick), jerk (why smooth onset, not peak magnitude, is the real craft), the four-axis tolerance envelope and the ASTM / EN standards that encode it for every body in the queue, and finally the compositional art of arranging forces in time — contrast, pacing, build and release — so each sensation lands.

13Forces & body

Positive g — the heavy moments, and why they are rationed

Analogy: Stand in a lift that suddenly accelerates upward and you feel briefly heavier — your knees sag, your stomach presses down. A coaster’s valley bottom is that lift turned up to eleven: the track flings you upward fast enough to feel several times your own weight. It is a core thrill — but blood obeys physics too, and that sets a hard ceiling on how much, and how long.

Positive g is the force you feel pressed into your seat — the sensation of increased weight — and on a coaster it appears wherever the track bends your path upward: the bottom of a drop’s pull-out, the bottom of a loop, the low point of any valley. The physics is the centripetal relation from the last track: at a valley of radius r taken at speed v, the track must push up with enough force both to hold you against gravity and to bend your path, giving a felt load of g-felt = 1 + v²/(r·g) — the ‘1’ is normal weight, the second term is the extra from the curve. The simulation lets you feel the levers: tighten the radius or raise the speed and the g climbs fast (v² again), and the gauge marches from the comfortable green band into the amber and red.

Why is it rationed? Because blood has mass and responds to g. Under sustained positive g, blood is pulled toward the feet and away from the brain and eyes; past roughly 4–5 g held for more than a few seconds, vision greys out (loss of colour, then tunnelling) and eventually blacks out as the eyes and brain are starved — the same physiology fighter pilots train and wear g-suits for. Coasters stay safe not by avoiding high g but by keeping it brief: a well-designed positive-g spike lasts a fraction of a second — long enough to thrill, far too short for blood to pool dangerously. Duration, not just magnitude, is the safety variable, which is why ride standards specify limits as g-versus-time envelopes, not single numbers.

This gives the designer a clear palette. Brief, high positive g (a snappy pull-out, the punch at a loop bottom) reads as powerful and exhilarating; moderate, sustained positive g (a long drawn-out helix) reads as a heavy, pressing ‘grey-out-adjacent’ intensity that must be watched carefully because it lasts; gentle positive g is the comfortable baseline most of a ride sits in. The lever for all of them is the radius: open it up and the same speed yields less g (gentler, more sustained-safe); tighten it and you get a sharper, stronger hit — which must then be kept short. Speed is usually fixed by the energy budget at that point, so radius is how the designer dials the feel.

The reflex: design positive g by its peak and its duration — brief spikes can be strong, sustained loads must stay modest — and use radius as the dial. A self-check: two pull-outs both momentarily hit 4.5 g, but one lasts 0.3 s and the other 4 s — which is the safety concern, and why? (The 4-second one — sustained ~4.5 g risks grey-out as blood pools, while the 0.3 s spike is over before the body responds. Magnitude alone does not tell you if a g-load is safe; you must know how long it is held.)

Positive g at a valley bottom climbs with speed and tightness — and the body’s tolerance band is fixed

g = 1 + v²/(r·g): tighten the radius or raise the speed and the gauge marches from the comfortable band toward red-out risk. Rides live in the green by keeping big loads brief — the safety limit is really g versus time, not a single number.

Watch: positive g — the heavy moments
positive gg = 1 + v²/(r·g)grey-out / red-outbrief spikes vs sustainedradius is the dial
14Forces & body

Airtime — the float, the lift, and the ejector

Analogy: Crest a humpback bridge too fast in a car and your stomach lifts, the seat drops away for an instant, loose change floats off the dash. That weightless half-second is airtime — the single most beloved sensation in coaster design — and it comes from the track falling away beneath you faster than gravity can pull you down with it.

Airtime is the feeling of reduced or negative weight — floating, or being lifted out of your seat — and it is produced over crests, where the track bends your path downward. The physics mirrors positive g with a sign flip: over a crest of radius r at speed v, the needed downward (centripetal) force is m·v²/r, and gravity supplies m·g of it, so the felt load is g-felt = 1 − v²/(r·g). When v²/(r·g) is small you feel merely light; when it equals 1 you are momentarily weightless (0 g); when it exceeds 1 the required force is more than gravity can supply, so the seat would leave you behind — you are flung up against your restraint. The simulation shows the body lifting off the seat as you tighten or speed up the crest.

Designers distinguish two flavours, and the difference is pure craft. Floater airtime (roughly 0 to −0.3 g) is gentle, sustained, dreamy — the long, high camelback where you hang suspended for a second, stomach aloft; it comes from a large-radius crest taken at moderate speed. Ejector airtime (strongly negative, −0.5 g and beyond) is sharp, violent, exhilarating — the feeling of being forcibly thrown from your seat; it comes from a tight-radius crest taken fast. Same sensation family, opposite texture: floater is a sigh, ejector is a gasp. Great airtime coasters deliberately mix them for rhythm — a big floater hill, then a sudden ejector pop.

Airtime is where restraints stop being a formality and start being load-bearing (the vehicles track). Under positive g the seat holds you; under negative g only the restraint keeps you in the vehicle, and the stronger the ejector, the more the restraint and the up-stop wheels (which keep the train itself on the track when it is pushed up and away from the rail) are doing real work. This is why ejector-heavy rides use restraints you can trust and why the restraint’s design is tuned to the airtime it must contain — enough to be safe, not so much that it kills the very floating feeling riders came for (over-restraint is a real way to ruin good airtime).

The reflex: produce airtime by bending the path down faster than gravity — large radius for floaty float, tight radius for ejector punch — and remember that under negative g the restraint and up-stop wheels are what hold rider and train. A self-check: a camelback taken at 70 km/h gives lovely floater airtime; the park wants sharper ejector there without changing the speed — what do you change, and what new concern appears? (Tighten the crest radius so v²/(r·g) exceeds 1 by more — but the stronger ejector now loads the restraint and up-stop wheels harder, and comfort/neck strain and restraint adequacy must be re-checked.)

Over a crest, bending the path down faster than gravity lifts you out of the seat — floater to ejector

g = 1 − v²/(r·g): a large, gentle crest gives floater airtime (0 to −0.3 g); tighten and speed it and you cross into ejector (−0.5 g and beyond), thrown against the restraint. Now the restraint and up-stop wheels — not the seat — hold you.

Watch: airtime — float and ejector
airtimeg = 1 − v²/(r·g)floater vs ejectorrestraint becomes load-bearingup-stop wheels
15Forces & body

Lateral g — the sideways force nobody loves (in excess)

Analogy: Take a roundabout too fast in a car and everyone slides toward the outer door, necks straining sideways — that unglamorous shove is lateral g. A little adds spice and a sense of speed; a lot just throws riders against each other and the restraint, tiring and uncomfortable. It is the force designers most often engineer away.

Lateral g is sideways force — the push you feel toward the outside of a horizontal turn — and it arises whenever the track curves left or right without tilting to compensate. On a flat (un-banked) turn of radius r at speed v, the rail must push you sideways with the full centripetal force, felt as a = v²/r of pure lateral g. Unlike positive g (press into seat, a familiar ‘heavy’) or airtime (lift, a sought-after ‘float’), lateral g is felt as being thrown off-axis — shoulder into the seat side, head wrenched sideways, body pressed against your neighbour or the restraint — and the human body tolerates and enjoys it far less than the in-line forces. Even modest sustained lateral g becomes tiring and, past a point, a neck-strain and comfort hazard.

Because the body dislikes it, lateral g is mostly something designers minimise or eliminate — and the tool is banking (the geometry track). By tilting the track into the turn, the designer rotates the net force so it points more into the seat (comfortable positive g) rather than sideways: at the ideal bank angle for a given speed and radius, the lateral g vanishes entirely and the rider feels only a smooth increase in downward seat pressure, however hard the turn. Most high-speed curves on modern coasters are heavily banked for exactly this reason — what looks like a dramatic tilt is the designer converting an uncomfortable sideways shove into a pleasant press.

So is lateral g always bad? No — small, deliberate amounts are a design flavour. A touch of un-banked or under-banked curve gives a sense of raw, unpolished speed and a ‘wild’ character — a signature of many classic wooden coasters, whose rattly, laterally-loose turns are part of their charm and are cherished by enthusiasts precisely because modern steel coasters bank it all away. The craft is intentionality: a small, chosen lateral kick for character is good design; large, accidental lateral g from an under-banked turn at speed is a comfort defect (and the classic cause of riders complaining a ride is ‘rough’ or that they got thrown around).

The reflex: treat lateral g as something to control deliberately — bank it out of high-speed turns for comfort, add a measured kick only where ‘wild’ is the intent — and never let it appear by accident. A self-check: a fast sweeping turn feels like it wrenches everyone sideways and riders complain of neck strain — what is the diagnosis and the fix, and what is the trade-off? (The turn is under-banked for its speed, so large lateral g appears; increase the bank angle toward the ideal for that v and r to convert the sideways force into seat pressure — the trade-off is purely geometric, needing the room and track-twist to bank, but comfort improves dramatically.)

Watch: lateral g — the sideways force
lateral ga = v²/r (sideways)banking cancels itdisliked in excessa measured kick for ‘wild’
16Forces & body

Jerk and onset — why smoothness is the real craft

Analogy: Two drivers can both brake from the same speed in the same distance, yet one stop leaves you nodding comfortably and the other snaps your head forward — the difference is how suddenly the braking arrived. On a coaster that suddenness is jerk, and managing it — not chasing peak g — is what separates a sublime ride from a ‘rough’ one.

Jerk is the rate of change of acceleration — how fast the g-force itself is rising or falling. It is the third derivative of position (after velocity and acceleration), and though it sounds academic it is intensely physical: a force that ramps in smoothly feels controlled and exhilarating, while the same peak force applied suddenly feels like a jolt, a wrench, a whiplash. Your body braces for forces it can anticipate; a high-jerk transition arrives before your muscles and neck can respond, so you are snapped rather than pressed. This is why ‘how rough is it?’ is often really a question about jerk, not about peak g — two rides pulling identical maximum g can feel worlds apart in comfort depending on how abruptly those loads turn on and off.

The villain is the abrupt change of curvature. If a straight section meets a circular curve directly, the centripetal force jumps from zero to its full value instantly at the join — a spike of near-infinite jerk, felt as a sharp jolt. The cure, borrowed from railway and highway engineering, is the transition (or spiral / easement) — a stretch of gradually tightening curvature that eases the force in smoothly, so acceleration ramps rather than steps. The clothoid (the geometry track’s star) is the mathematically ideal such transition: its curvature increases linearly with distance, giving constant, gentle jerk. Nearly every smooth element on a modern coaster is built from these easements, not bare circles.

This is why loops are teardrops, not circles (previewed here, detailed in the geometry track): a circular loop would slam the rider with a huge, abrupt g-spike at the bottom where it meets the straight track; the clothoid teardrop eases the curvature in and out, spreading the force and taming the jerk, which is what made modern inversions comfortable enough to become ubiquitous. And it is why a worn or poorly-shaped element feels ‘rough’ — wheel wear, track settling, or a crude original geometry create little abrupt curvature changes that read as rattles and jolts. Smoothness is not softness; it is controlled onset.

The reflex: design for the onset of force, not just its peak — ease every element in and out with transitions so jerk stays low, and suspect jerk (not magnitude) whenever a ride feels ‘rough.’ This is the quiet craft that enthusiasts feel but cannot always name, and it is where a master designer’s touch shows. A self-check: a ride’s loop pulls a perfectly reasonable peak g, yet riders report a jarring ‘slam’ entering it — what is the likely cause and the fix? (Too-abrupt a curvature change at the loop entry — high jerk — even though the peak g is fine; the fix is a proper clothoid easement into the loop so the force ramps in smoothly rather than hitting all at once.)

Watch: jerk and the onset of force
jerk (rate of change of g)smooth onset vs jolttransitions / easementsclothoid = constant jerk‘rough’ usually means jerk
17Forces & body

The tolerance envelope — peak, duration, direction, and the standards

Analogy: A doctor does not clear you for exercise with a single number but with a whole chart — heart rate against time, for your age, in each direction of stress. Ride forces are cleared the same way: not ‘how many g?’ but a full envelope of magnitude versus duration, per direction, for the range of human bodies — and that envelope is written into formal safety standards.

Everything in this track combines into the acceleration envelope: the designed-and-regulated boundary of what forces may be applied to a rider, and it has four axes, not one. Magnitude (how many g). Duration (for how long — the crucial second axis, since brief spikes are safe where sustained loads are not, as the positive-g scenario showed). Direction (the body tolerates forces very differently depending on axis: ‘eyeballs-in/out’ fore-aft, ‘eyeballs-down’ positive/head-to-foot which causes grey-out, ‘eyeballs-up’ negative/foot-to-head which is far less tolerated and causes red-out, and lateral — each has its own limits). And implicitly onset / jerk, the rate the force arrives. A ride is safe only if its forces stay inside this multi-axis envelope everywhere, for every rider.

These limits are not folklore; they are codified in amusement-ride safety standards that designers must meet. In North America, ASTM F24 (the F2291 design standard among them) specifies acceleration limits as magnitude-versus-duration curves for each direction; in Europe, EN 13814 plays the equivalent role; other regions have their own, often harmonised. These documents encode decades of biomechanical research into concrete envelopes — e.g. higher briefly-tolerable g in the ‘into-seat’ direction, much tighter limits for negative (head-to-foot-reversed) and lateral loads, and explicit duration bands. Designing a ride includes proving, by simulation and measurement, that its computed and measured accelerations stay within these curves — a core part of the safety case (testing track).

The envelope must also cover the range of human bodies, which is why it connects to restraints, seating, and ride-access rules. The same force acts differently on a child and an adult, a slight and a large frame; restraints and seat geometry are designed so that everyone the ride admits experiences forces within safe limits and stays properly contained, and height, age, or health requirements exist precisely to exclude bodies the envelope cannot safely cover (very small children for whom restraints do not fit or whose necks cannot take the loads; riders for whom the physiological stress is contraindicated). The posted rules at a ride’s entrance are the visible edge of its acceleration envelope.

The reflex: treat rider forces as a four-axis envelope — magnitude, duration, direction, onset — prove the whole ride stays inside the governing standard’s curves for every admitted body, and use restraints and access rules to guarantee it. A self-check: why do standards permit a higher g limit in the ‘pressed into the seat’ direction than in the ‘lifted up out of the seat’ or sideways directions? (The body — its blood flow, spine, and neck — tolerates head-to-foot ‘eyeballs-down’ loading far better than negative/lateral loading; the heart can push blood against moderate positive g briefly, whereas negative g pools blood in the head (red-out) and lateral/neck loading is poorly supported — so the safe envelope is directional.)

Watch: the force tolerance envelope and standards
magnitude × durationdirectional limitsASTM F24 / EN 13814prove it by sim & measurementrestraints & access rules
18Forces & body

Composing the force sequence — sensation as music over a whole ride

Analogy: A song that is all chorus exhausts you; one that is all verse bores you. The pleasure is in the arrangement — build, drop, breath, surprise, resolve. A ride’s forces are composed the same way: not a pile of big moments but a sequence, paced so each sensation lands because of what came before and after. This is where force engineering becomes an art.

The individual forces — positive g, airtime, lateral — are the notes; the sequence is the music, and a great ride is distinguished less by any single element than by how its elements are arranged in time. The guiding idea is contrast and pacing: the human nervous system habituates — a fifth big drop in a row thrills less than the first — so sensations must vary to keep landing. A masterful layout alternates: a powerful positive-g pull-out, then floaty airtime, then a sweeping banked turn, then a sudden ejector pop, then a breather, then a surprise. Each moment is set up by contrast with its neighbours; the airtime feels higher right after heavy g, the quiet makes the next hit louder.

Good composition has a recognisable dramatic arc, borrowed straight from storytelling (and the story track makes this explicit). The lift hill is anticipation — slow, building dread, the clack of the chain, the view; the first drop is the release — the biggest single payoff, placed first because the energy is greatest there; the mid-course develops and surprises — varied elements, a hidden moment, a change of pace; and the finale resolves — often a last burst or a satisfying run to the brakes. Pacing a breather or two is not wasted track; it is the rest between phrases that makes the next crescendo hit. Rides that are relentless wall-to-wall intensity often feel less thrilling than well-paced ones, because nothing stands out.

Composition also respects the physiological reality the envelope imposes, turning constraint into craft. You cannot sustain high g, so you punctuate with it; you cannot hold strong ejector, so you pop it and move on; you must stay in the envelope, so you sequence brief peaks with recovery between — which, happily, is also what makes the ride feel best. The constraint and the art point the same way: the envelope wants brief, varied, well-spaced forces, and so does a thrilled rider. The designer composes within the envelope, not against it, which is why the best rides feel both wild and effortless.

The reflex, and the summation of the forces track: compose the ride as a sequence of forces — contrast, pace, build, release, surprise, resolve — using brief varied peaks within the human envelope, so each sensation lands because of its neighbours. A self-check: a new coaster has five huge, nearly identical airtime hills back to back and testers say it is ‘oddly boring for how intense it is’ — what is the compositional problem and one fix? (No contrast or pacing — the nervous system habituates to the repeated identical sensation, so later hills under-deliver; vary them (mix floater and ejector, change heights), insert a different element (a turn, a tunnel, a near-miss) or a brief breather between, so each airtime moment regains its impact.)

Forces & the body, in one breath —Every thrill is inertia resisting the track, felt as positive g (heavy, rationed by duration), airtime (float to ejector, restraint-borne), or lateral g (banked away, or a measured ‘wild’ kick). What makes it feel good is low jerk (smooth onset) and composition (contrast and pacing) — all kept inside a four-axis tolerance envelope (magnitude × duration × direction × onset) written into ASTM F24 / EN 13814, for every body in the queue.
Watch: composing the force sequence
sensation as musiccontrast & pacingdramatic arc (build-release)habituation → vary itcompose within the envelope
04

Track geometry — the curve that is the ride

The energy and forces of the earlier tracks are realised by one thing: the precise 3-D path the vehicle follows — so the geometry is the designer’s primary surface of control. This track covers that surface in depth: why the track is the ride and is designed as one continuous curve; the clothoid that gives smooth force onset and turns loops into teardrops; banking that rotates a turn’s sideways shove into comfortable seat pressure; the heartline that lets you design around the rider’s body rather than the rail; the transitions and 3-D continuity that stitch named elements into one seamless gesture (and stage near-misses for drama); and the surveying and fabrication craft that turns a millimetre-precise mathematical curve into accurate steel in the ground.

19Track geometry

The track is the ride — geometry as the primary design surface

Analogy: A sculptor does not add the beauty afterward; it is the shape of the stone. A coaster’s experience is not added to the track — it is the track. The exact three-dimensional curve the train follows produces every force, every sensation, every moment of the ride. Change the curve and you change the ride; the geometry is the design, and everything else serves it.

The energy budget and the forces of the last two tracks are realised by one thing: the precise 3-D path the vehicle follows. The track’s shape at every point — its slope (setting how PE trades to KE), its curvature (setting the centripetal force, hence the g), its bank angle (setting how that force splits into seat-pressure versus sideways), and how all three change along the path (setting the jerk) — is the ride experience, computed point by point. This is why the track geometry is the designer’s primary surface of control: you do not design ‘forces’ directly, you design a curve, and the forces fall out of it. Master the geometry and you master the ride.

What makes this hard is that the path must satisfy many demands simultaneously and continuously. At every point it must: deliver the intended force (right curvature and bank for the speed there), stay within the energy budget (right slope given what is left), transition smoothly from the previous element (low jerk), fit the available site (land, height limits, existing structures, the station location), keep clear of itself and neighbours (clearance envelopes — no near-miss that is an actual miss), and remain buildable in steel and structurally sound. A coaster layout is the solution to all of these at once, which is why it is designed as a single continuous mathematical curve, not a sequence of independent parts bolted together.

The modern practice is thoroughly computational and parametric. Designers build the track as mathematical curves (often splines through control points, with curvature and bank defined as functions along the path), and specialised software computes the resulting speed, g-forces, and jerk at every point in real time — the ride-design-software loop (track 17). The designer sculpts the curve — raise this crest, open that radius, roll in this bank — and immediately sees the force profile respond, iterating until the ride reads right and stays within envelopes and the site. The output is a geometry precise enough to manufacture: coordinates and orientations the fabricator turns directly into bent steel.

The reflex: design the curve, and let the forces, energy, and feel follow from it — because the geometry is not a container for the ride, it is the ride. Everything in this track is a tool for shaping that curve well: the clothoid for smooth, humane force onset; banking for turning sideways force into seat pressure; the heartline for designing around the rider; transitions for stitching elements; and surveying for getting the designed curve built accurately in the real world. A self-check: why can two coasters with identical lift height, top speed, and element list still feel completely different? (Because the geometry — the exact radii, bank angles, transition shapes, and how curvature changes — differs; same ingredients, different curve, different forces and jerk, different ride. The feel lives in the shape, not the list.)

geometry is the designslope / curvature / bank / changemany constraints at onceparametric curves + live forcesculpt the curve, forces follow
20Track geometry

The clothoid — why loops are teardrops, not circles

Analogy: Merge onto a motorway and the on-ramp does not join the highway at a sudden corner — it curves in gradually, tightening smoothly until you are up to speed and aligned. That gentle easing-in spiral is a clothoid, and it is exactly why a modern coaster loop is a teardrop: it eases the turn in and out so the force builds gently instead of slamming you.

A clothoid (also called an Euler spiral or Cornu spiral) is a curve whose curvature increases linearly with distance along it — it starts straight (zero curvature, infinite radius) and tightens at a steady rate. That single property makes it the ideal transition: because centripetal acceleration is v²/r and the radius changes smoothly rather than jumping, the g-force ramps in at a constant, gentle rate — constant jerk (the forces track). Railways and highways have used clothoid easements for over a century for exactly this reason: to avoid the lurch of a straight meeting a circle directly. Coasters inherited the idea, and it transformed them.

The famous application is the vertical loop. A circular loop is a disaster ergonomically: to get a rider over the top with enough speed, the circle must be small, but a small radius at the fast bottom (where v is highest) produces, via v²/r, a crushing g-spike — early circular loops pulled brutal, dangerous forces and battered riders. The clothoid teardrop solves it with varying radius: large radius low down where the train is fast (so v²/r stays moderate — gentle g at the bottom) and tight radius up top where the train is slow (so there is still enough force to hold the rider in with positive g at the apex). The simulation shows the contrast directly: the circle’s peak g is brutal; the teardrop’s is tamed, while the top still keeps you pinned.

This is one of the pivotal innovations in coaster history — the modern looping coaster was essentially unlocked by applying clothoid geometry, because it made inversions comfortable and safe enough to run all day for the general public. And the principle generalises far beyond loops: every smooth element uses clothoid-like transitions at its entry and exit — the easement into a drop’s pull-out, the spiral into and out of a turn, the shaping of a crest — so that curvature (and therefore force) always ramps rather than steps. When you hear that a ride is ‘glassy smooth,’ you are hearing good transition geometry; when a ride is ‘rough,’ its curvature changes too abruptly somewhere.

The reflex: never join curves abruptly — ease every element in and out with clothoid transitions so curvature, and thus force, changes gradually; and shape loops as teardrops to tame the fast-bottom g-spike while keeping the slow top positive. A self-check: a designer sketches a loop as a perfect circle to ‘keep it simple’ — what two problems appear, and how does the teardrop fix both? (A circle gives a brutal g-spike at the fast bottom and, if sized to be gentle there, too little force to hold riders in at the top; the teardrop uses a large bottom radius to tame the bottom g and a tight top radius to keep positive g at the apex — varying radius solves both at once.)

A circular loop slams the rider at the fast bottom; the clothoid teardrop tames it while keeping the top positive

Curvature rising smoothly means the g ramps rather than steps. The teardrop uses a large radius low down (gentle g where the train is fast) and a tight radius up top (enough force to hold you in where it is slow) — the geometry that made loops humane.
